TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

The highlights this week: Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi and Chinese President Xi Jinping meet on the sidelines of the BRICS summit , Bangladesh faces the world’s worst measles outbreak as the death toll passes 1,000, and thousands of people remain missing after Nepal’s deadly floods . Over the weekend, Chinese President Xi Jinping traveled to India for the first time in seven years. Xi was in New Delhi for 24 hours to attend this year’s BRICS leaders’ summit, where he held a meeting with Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi. An Indian Foreign Ministry statement said the two leaders pledged to strengthen commercial and cultural cooperation. A more striking readout came from Chinese Foreign Minister Wang Yi, who said that the bilateral meeting covered long-term, strategic issues and that Modi and Xi reached “the most important consensus” to be partners, not rivals. Beijing hopes the meeting will advance bilateral ties “without stagnation or backsliding,” Wang added. These are positive words for a relationship between major competitors—and one that was plunged into deep crisis after a deadly 2020 border clash in the Ladakh region. But an India-China rapprochement has been playing out for nearly two years . Xi’s visit marked the culmination, not the commencement, of India-China detente. Still, the extent of the thaw shouldn’t be overstated. Historically, India-China ties have had their moments, with ample heady talk and good optics. Invariably, fresh tensions have spoiled them; the two are destined to remain rivals by history, geography, and geopolitics. In October 2024, the post-Ladakh tensions began to ease with the inking of a new border agreement . India and China subsequently made pledges to resume direct air flights and bolster commercial cooperation . In March 2025, Modi said on a podcast that cooperation with China was “essential” for global stability. Contrary to some commentary , the detente began before U.S. President Donald Trump was elected to a second term and wasn’t sparked by his policies. With Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney seated on her left, von der Leyen outlined what such a future might look like, starting with an expansion of their existing free-trade agreement. Carney has been pitching such an arrangement to European officials for months. A Canadian official told The Associated Press last week that the two sides are discussing potential ties that stop just short of full membership. This could include allowing Canadian citizens to live and work in EU member states without needing a visa. An expanded partnership might also bolster collaboration in defense production, critical minerals procurement, economic security, and the development of artificial intelligence, among others. Canada wants “to get as close as we possibly can” to the bloc, Jonathan Wilkinson, Canada’s ambassador-designate to the EU, said on Wednesday, adding that further discussions are expected to take place at the Canada-EU summit in October. Under EU regulations, the bloc requires unanimous approval to welcome new members. Although von der Leyen insisted on Wednesday that the Canada proposal is “not a partnership against anyone else,” the gesture comes at a time when both Canada’s and Europe’s relations with the United States under President Donald Trump are greatly strained . Importantly, the public is paying close attention to how candidates intend to address widespread violence and kidnappings for ransom. A mass kidnapping of schoolchildren in Tinubu’s political heartland in the southwestern state of Oyo in May magnified the issue. Did I insult anyone in particular, or what?” His summons is part of a national campaign that has turned Georgia’s courts into enforcers of speech control. In February, the Georgian parliament adopted amendments to Georgia’s Soviet-era Code of Administrative Offenses and added provisions punishing verbal insults, swearing, taunting, and other offensive acts directed at political officeholders, civil servants, and public employees. A first offense carries a fine of between 1,500 and 4,000 lari or up to 45 days in administrative detention. A repeat offense can bring a fine of up to 6,000 lari or between five and 60 days behind bars. In Georgia, the state-led campaign against insults and social media speech forms one layer of a larger democratic collapse
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/09/16/georgia-vulgarity-social-media-government/

Late last month, OpenAI revealed a worrisome new development: Not only had one of its models broken out of its “sandbox” and hacked the company Hugging Face, it had in fact done so in secret coordination with hundreds of other OpenAI models (or “agents”). Subsequent reports revealed that OpenAI’s agents had previously infiltrated two other websites months before the Hugging Face incident. The hacks exacerbated long-standing concerns around the growing cyber-capabilities of advanced AI models, including their ability to find vulnerabilities in software systems and the potential for AI-assisted bad actors to more easily disrupt poorly defended critical infrastructure such as water systems . The same day as the OpenAI report, Microsoft co-founder Bill Gates published an essay warning about the “turbulent AI era” and highlighting societal risks from the technology such as widespread job losses, diminished human cognition and relationships, and use by a variety of bad actors to do harm. “For as long as I can remember, I’ve wished innovation could happen faster,” he wrote. “With AI, my feelings are more complicated.” But even those warnings were dwarfed by the highly public resignation last week of Jacob Coxon, a researcher at the AI firm Anthropic who previously worked at OpenAI. “Neither company is acting responsibly,” Coxon wrote in a viral social media post announcing his resignation. “The people building AI earnestly believe that it could kill us all by the end of the decade.” Coxon’s sentiments were echoed by several current employees at Anthropic, including Evan Hubinger, who leads the company’s research on alignment (making sure AI models are aligned with humans). “J

With Washington still publicly calling for denuclearization and Pyongyang rejecting the possibility, renewed talks would test whether the two sides can negotiate limits on North Korea’s arsenal without agreeing on its eventual dismantlement. A meeting with Trump would offer Kim another chance to present his daughter on the global stage, associating his potential heir with the prestige of a direct dealing with a U.S. president. * Lebanon’s New Amnesty Law Shows Sunnis’ Growing Power
  Foreign Policy | 16 Sep 13:21 UTC
  How regional developments helped Lebanese Islamists get out of jail.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. On Aug. 12, Lebanon’s parliament passed a general amnesty , the first since 1991. The law technically aims to address overcrowding in prisons. But, as with every policy debate in Lebanon, its stated aims exist alongside a second layer of sectarian politics. Seen in this light, the amnesty also reflects a subtle shift in the balance of power toward Lebanon’s Sunnis. The concrete impact of the new law will be reducing prison terms for or releasing thousands of prisoners. Lebanon’s prisons are indeed overcrowded, with many inmates awaiting their trials for years. Now, death sentences will be commuted to life sentences, life sentences will be reduced to 17 prison years (one prison year is measured as nine months), and inmates detained for more than 12 prison years without charges will automatically be released. On Aug. 12, Lebanon’s parliament passed a general amnesty , the first since 1991. The law technically aims to address overcrowding in prisons. But, as with every policy debate in Lebanon, its stated aims exist alongside a second layer of sectarian politics. Seen in this light, the amnesty also reflects a subtle shift in the balance of power toward Lebanon’s Sunnis. The concrete impact of the new law will be reducing prison terms for or releasing thousands of prisoners. Lebanon’s prisons are indeed overcrowded, with many inmates awaiting their trials for years. Now, death sentences will be commuted to life sentences, life sentences will be reduced to 17 prison years (one prison year is measured as nine months), and inmates detained for more than 12 prison years without charges will automatically be released. From the perspective of relieving overcrowding and preventing people from being held without due process, this legislation makes sense. But there remains the inevitably sectarian question of who gets pardoned and why. Every faith community is looking out for its own type of criminal. For years, the Shiite group Hezbollah has been demanding amnesty for cannabis and car theft offenses because core Hezbollah constituencies tend to dominate these crimes. Christian parties want pardons for the families of those who collaborated with the Israeli occupation and fled to Israel when it ended in 2000. Hezbollah has long had the leverage to secure its political interests through loyalists including in military intelligence and the General Security Directorate . The group’s ability to shape the amnesty law is noteworthy
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/09/16/lebanon-beirut-islamist-sunni-hezbollah-syria-amnesty/

* Analysis: India’s power-sector emissions flat for two years due to clean-energy surge
  Carbon Brief | 16 Sep 23:01 UTC
  A surge in clean energy has kept carbon dioxide (CO2) emissions in check across India’s power sector, with no growth… The post Analysis: India’s power-sector emissions flat for two years due to clean-energy surge appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: A surge in clean energy has kept carbon dioxide (CO2) emissions in check across India’s power sector, with no growth from the first half of 2024 to the same period in 2026. L auri Myllyvirta, lead analyst at Centre for Research on Energy and Clean Air (CREA) This is the first time in more than 50 years that there has been no growth in India’s coal power over a two-year period, even as electricity demand grew overall. At the same time, both oil and gas consumption have fallen across the nation for two years in a row, helping alleviate the shock of the Hormuz crisis . Nevertheless, the new six-monthly analysis for Carbon Brief shows that India’s emissions grew by 3.7% year-on-year in the first half of 2026, due to increases from steel, cement and other sectors. Other key findings for the first half of 2026 include: If the pace of India’s clean-energy expansion is to continue, it will need to upgrade its electricity grid, rapidly build out energy storage and boost the flexibility of coal power. While clean-energy expansion is covering most or all of India’s power-demand growth, the fossil-fuel industry continues to pursue major capital investments. This includes large amounts of new coal-power capacity , ambitious plans for the conversion of coal-to-chemicals and efforts to boost domestic coking coal production for the steel sector. While CO2 output from the power sector is flat, with oil and gas in decline, India’s emissions still went up due to the contribution from industry. India lags behind its competitors – including most large emerging economies – when it comes to electrifying its industrial sector. Faster progress would enable clean electricity to substitute for fossil fuels in industry, as well as for power, offering the potential for India to cut its emissions overall. Last year, India’s CO2 emissions from fossil fuels and cement grew at their slowest pace in two decades, according to previous analysis for Carbon Brief . This sharp slowdown was due to rapid clean-energy growth and flat oil demand, combined with rising emissions from steel and cement. The first half of 2026 marks a continuation of these trends. Most strikingly, the ongoing surge in clean-energy generation means that emissions have flatlined in India’s power sector for two years, as shown in the figure below. Power-sector CO2 was the same in the first half of 2026 as two years earlier, with a small decline in 2025 having been reversed over the same period this year. Beyond electricity
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/analysis-indias-power-sector-emissions-flat-for-two-years-due-to-clean-energy-surge

* Analysis: Global fossil-fuel emissions set to fall in 2026 amid Hormuz crisis
  Carbon Brief | 16 Sep 13:34 UTC
  Global fossil-fuel emissions are set to fall by around 0.5% in 2026 amid the fallout from the Hormuz crisis, according… The post Analysis: Global fossil-fuel emissions set to fall in 2026 amid Hormuz crisis appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: Global fossil-fuel emissions are set to fall by around 0.5% in 2026 amid the fallout from the Hormuz crisis, according to Carbon Brief analysis. The US-Iran war has severely disrupted trade through the strait of Hormuz, causing a spike in oil and gas prices that continues to ripple around the global economy. Each month of disruption – and each new flashpoint, such as in Yemen – is increasing the incentive to switch to alternatives. Those alternatives include coal, with the latest forecasts pointing to a 1.2% rise in coal demand this year – apparently supporting media claims of a “ return to coal ” in the wake of the crisis. Yet Carbon Brief’s analysis shows the rise in emissions associated with this increased coal use, much of which is unrelated to Hormuz, is set to be more than offset by declines for oil and gas. The estimated overall impact on carbon dioxide (CO2) emissions from fossil fuels in 2026 is shown in the figure below and amounts to a reduction of around 0.5% from 2025 levels. (Fossil fuels account for two-thirds of global greenhouse gas emissions.) The emissions estimates for each fossil fuel are based on the latest forecasts from the International Energy Agency (IEA) for coal , oil and gas , in light of the ongoing global energy crisis. For example, the agency initially estimated that global coal demand would decline this year . In its 2025 coal report, published in mid-December, it said that declining coal demand in China would outweigh the impact of pro-coal policies under US president Donald Trump. In contrast, the latest update , published in September 2026, said that global coal demand would rise by 1.2% in 2026, instead of the small decline that had been expected. The report highlighted the boost to coal demand from higher gas prices in the wake of Hormuz. However, there are limits to this, because few countries can switch from gas to coal at large scale. The IEA’s latest report also noted the role of a strong El Niño , which is pushing up the need for cooling and depressing hydropower output in key markets. Other short-term factors are also affecting coal demand this year, including a rising amount of “ wasted ” wind and solar in China. For gas, the IEA did not initially update its previous forecast that global gas demand would rise by 2.0% in 2026, which had been published in January of this year. Its most recent forecast – published in July – already pointed to a 0.6% drop in demand in 2026. Since then, pressure on gas demand from hi

--- Editorial (35) ---

* A True-Crime Producer Posed as an Heiress — Then Allegedly Stole $30 Million
  Longreads | 16 Sep 17:08 UTC
  "Mary Carole McDonnell made low-budget crime shows, but her own story was built on deceptions fit for one of her series. Now, she’s on the FBI’s Most Wanted list and the target of a global manhunt."
  FULL TEXT: The FBI isn’t the only interested party searching for Mary Carole McDonnell, the 74-year-old producer behind TV series like I Married a Murderer . There are the former employees who allege millions in unpaid wages, and the two sons who claim their mother forged their signatures on bank documents. There’s an entrepreneur in the United Arab Emirates who accuses her of “mind games,” and the former bank vice-president turned true-crime podcaster who’d like to make a documentary about McDonnell. Cheyenne Roundtree goes deep on the woman who, nearly a decade ago, walked into a California bank with a pecan pie and left with a $15 million loan before vanishing. The story has all the makings of a viral documentary replete with breathless details, similar to the ones McDonnell’s company produced. Her backstory is littered with half-truths and deceptions, including a lie that her mentally disabled younger sister was murdered after being kidnapped. Questions remain on how she was able to pull off such a brazen heist. And it has sparked an ongoing hunt among the FBI, banking institutions, a true-crime podcaster, and television producers to see who can unravel McDonnell’s tangled history, track her down in a foreign land, recoup the financial losses she caused, and perhaps bring the unbelievable tale to screens. “A century ago, an aspiring actress published a remarkable autobiography. She made up most of it.” “High-speed accidents, crooked lawyers, and poor people desperate for cash—it was the kind of scheme that could have been cooked up only in the Big Easy.” “How a team of sleuths reportedly tracked down the Brad Pitt impersonators who cyber-scammed a middle-aged Frenchwoman out of her life’s savings.”
  https://longreads.com/2026/09/16/mary-carole-mcdonnell-dirty-money-moves-podcast/

The move, announced in response to Iranian strikes on Emirati ships, is seen as part of the Trump administration’s strategy of economically strangling Iran. But Iranian watermelons, and much more, are still flowing into Dubai’s markets, feeding its restaurants, supermarkets and hotels, and providing one small example of how difficult it would be to fully separate the two countries’ historically linked economies. Although the Strait of Hormuz has today become a geopolitical choke point, blocking the flow of oil, fertilizer and global trade, the area has long been a local hub of economic and cultural exchange. For centuries, merchants from Iran and the Arabian Peninsula sailed across the Gulf on wooden dhows, ferrying saffron, pearls, spices and pottery. Over the years, communities in the ports of Dubai, Bandar Abbas and Bandar Lengeh became a melting pot of Persian and Arabic culture, speaking both languages. Despite the ongoing conflict, this ancient trade is still visible in Dubai. In the old town, impressively large turquoise and white dhows parade the port at Marfa Wharf, cranes loading their cargo, soon ready to sail. In 1979, the Iranian Revolution sparked a huge influx of wealthy Iranian business owners and educated professionals fleeing the new Islamic Republic. Today the UAE is home to some 560,000 Iranians, the vast majority in Dubai. They own businesses, buy property and employ workers, representing a cornerstone of the economy. The two models will cost the highest in Turkey, nearly double the U.S. price, due to local taxes, Counterpoint says. In India — iPhone’s fourth-largest market and the second-largest manufacturing hub for Apple — prices will be 30% to 40% higher than in the U.S. for similar reasons. The steep price tags persist despite Apple’s continued efforts to diversify manufacturing operations to reduce dependence on China. India now accounts for about 25% of Apple’s total global iPhone production, but the new foldable model Duo is not being produced in the country. “India pricing is steep, and the difference is primarily due to the fact that initial Duo sales will not be ‘Made in India’ for some time,” Tarun Pathak, director of devices and ecosystems at Counterpoint Research, told Rest of World . “Consequently, imported units attract full import duty — making them significantly more expensive — along with GST [goods and services tax] and the impact of a weaker rupee.” The U.S., as always, continues to have the most affordable prices in the world for the latest launches. The second-cheapest market, the UAE, has a relatively smaller price tag not just for the iPhone but also for other brands and models, due to a low value-added tax at 5% and no other taxes. 25% of Apple’s global iPhone production is now from India. In China and Korea, prices skew lower not only due to different tax rates but also because of robust local market competition. “China is highly competitive with Huawei, Oppo, Vivo, while Samsung is the heavyweight in Korea,” Harshit Rastogi, senior analyst at Counterpoint Research, told Rest of World . Even the made-in-India iPhones are still largely only assembled in the country. Only 18% to 20% of electronic components originate domestically, while the rest are imported and taxed heavily. “The Pro models have even higher import dependence than the base model,” Rastogi said. “As a greater share of components becomes locally sourced in the coming years, price increases are likely to become more gradual.” In June, Apple’s former CEO Tim Cook warned that the price of existing models would go up due to a shortage of chips.   FULL TEXT: All original watercolours by Emily Faccini When he was a child growing up in Emilia-Romagna, my great-grandfather Marco once found an eagle fledgling that had fallen from its nest. He kept it warm in the folds of his shirt and ran home, only to discover as he undressed that the bird had eaten strips of his flesh, its beak tinged red with blood. The rescued eagle thrived in my great-grandfather’s care, its wingspan soon a remarkable length. And all the while, Marco himself grew immensely tall. Then, one day, without warning, the eagle flew away. The moment my father told me this story when I was a boy, it lodged itself deep in my imagination. I pictured young Marco as a giant striding through the forests of north-central Italy, calling to the bird of prey, waiting in vain for it to land on his outstretched hand. Marco merged with the heroes of my illustrated classical civilisation books, earning his place between the busts of Roman emperors and the chapters on Greek mythology. What I never realised was that this family legend held clues to who I am and to my relationship to Italy. L ina Stores deli in Soho, London, has been a pillar of Italian life in England for decades. It’s where we’d sometimes meet our paternal grandparents when visiting London from our home in France. My brothers and I would line up in front of the refrigerated counter and ask for our favourite ravioli, doing our utmost to avoid any slip-ups in Italian that would make the staff wince: ‘ ricotta e spinaci ’, ‘ zucca ’, ‘ funghi porcini ’. Back at our grandparents’ flat, Nonna served the ravioli on an elaborately laid table, and my grandfather appeared with freshly grated Parmigiano. He’d sprinkle the cheese evenly over our pasta; never too much, nor too little. How he did this, and with such speed, we didn’t know. If we had spaghetti, Nonna would wrap her right hand around ours and twirl the fork with us so we held just the right amount, the bespattered tablecloth damning proof of our slapdash habits at home. My grandparents trusted that these rituals of the table (they saw them as basic codes of Italianness, connecting displaced Italians everywhere) would allow us to paper over the differences in our ethnically blended and peripatetic family. In this way, we’d be able to claim Italian identity for ourselves, despite its competition in our lives with Englishness and Frenchness. I fall into it and remember that even if I fail in a great number of things, on any given day, I can love my friends well just by finding something they love and saying, “Look, I saw this and I thought of you.” “The modern world has made us ill-equipped for the nuisances of past technologies, even as it has fuelled nostalgia for things that might transport us back to calmer times.” “The feeling is most commonly framed as an end point, a level of despondency that cannot be overcome. Hayden has now seen and reported from conflicts across continents, from Iraq, to Rwanda, to Ukraine. * The bombarding of childhood
  Aeon | 14 Sep 10:00 UTC
  It’s not just screen time: the problem with children’s media is that it has become relentlessly hectic, loud and bright - by Natalia Kucirkova Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: I am at the airport gate waiting to depart, watching passengers hurry past with rolling suitcases and paper cups of coffee. A small boy sits very still beside his mother. He holds an iPad close to his chest, almost in a cramp, clutching the screen as if it might otherwise run away. I hear loud clapping hands and squeaking children’s voices. I lean over and see a cartoon character in green snatch a toy from another character in pink. The pink character begins to cry, tears splashing from enormous eyes like sprinkles. The green character shouts sorry. Instantly, the tears stop and a wide smile replaces the tears before I have blinked. And, before I have even worked out whether the characters are speaking English or Arabic (as the mother does to the boy), the scene has changed and the two characters are rowing a boat with a random song in the background. The boy’s eyes dart on the screen from right to left, centre, down. His body is still but his attention is being hurried from cue to cue. This is the problem I have come to think of as the pace of children’s media. Children’s media are usually judged by their content and quantity: how many minutes watched and whether it was educational, age-appropriate, diverse, prosocial, commercial or safe. These questions matter and, with my colleagues, I have studied them through experiments and contributed to guidelines, regulations, or toolkits for designers. But we have been missing a bigger design feature that may be just as consequential: the time a medium gives a child to make meaning. A programme or app can contain good lessons and still move too quickly for those lessons to settle. For instance, in the US animated educational series Ask the StoryBots (2016-20), the subject matter may be genuinely rich – why the sky is blue, how eyes work, or where planets come from – but the explanation unfolds through rapid jokes, songs, visual shifts and sudden changes in style. Before a child has time to sit with one image or idea, the scene has moved on to the next, with a burst of information. Different media have different temporal demands: a digital picture book, a game, a television episode and an app each ask a child to attend differently. But, across media, the important question is whether the pace suits the experience that the narrative claims to support. Does it give children time to follow a story, grasp a concept, identify with a character, regulate emotion or share attention with another person? Or does it continua
  https://aeon.co/essays/its-not-just-screen-time-kids-are-bombarded-by-hectic-media

* India’s ‘Freebie’ Politics May Have Reached America
  New Lines | 11 Sep 17:02 UTC
  Trump’s $5,000 “dividend” to Americans if Republicans retain Congress sounds outrageous in the U.S. But across the developing democratic world, election-time cash and freebies have long been entrenched in politics. India offers a cautionary precedent. The post India’s ‘Freebie’ Politics May Have Reached America appeared first on New Lines...
  FULL TEXT: (Photo by: Arun Sankar/AFP via Getty Images) In a Trumpian world, shock value is a depreciating currency, even for the man at its center. This week, however, U.S. President Donald Trump managed to spend some anyway, pledging a $5,000 “Trump dividend” to every adult American citizen if Republicans retain control of Congress come November. The news expectedly drew outrage, with critics calling the stunt a “bribe” and pointing to the president’s earlier unfulfilled promises of similar dividends, but across large swaths of the developing democratic world, it landed less as a scandal than a recognition of a long-entrenched practice. The line between a state benefit and vote-buying payouts shouldn’t be blurry, but in countries such as India, the Philippines, Kenya, Egypt and Indonesia, the practice is so widespread that voters no longer find flaw in politicians handing out cash or staples like rice, oil and sugar during election cycles, or after. In fact, Indian politicians have graduated from cash to a full dowry — cows, goats, laptops, bicycles, blenders, wristwatches, even gold, drugs and alcohol — as if courting the electorate required a trousseau. The 2019 national election saw the country’s electoral commission seize cash and goods worth roughly $475 million distributed to buy votes; by the 2024 election, that figure had more than doubled to over $1 billion. In Kenya, too, cash and in-kind gifts have been distributed openly at election rallies, while in Egypt, party workers have reportedly paid people roughly up to $5 outside polling sites, often topping it with a box of groceries and tickets to a Cairo amusement park. Indonesians have called this “politik uang,” or “money politics.” Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi coined a term of his own — “revdi culture” — using the name of the north Indian sweet as a stand-in for “freebies.” American political language has no equivalent shorthand for any of this yet. Even as Modi has attempted to shame both voters and political parties for this indulgence, his own Bharatiya Janata Party (BJP) hasn’t been able to resist the temptation. With a government payment system in place, India’s version of payouts lately comes disguised as public cash transfer schemes targeted particularly at women, promised on the campaign trail in the hope of winning votes, and rolled out once the party assumes power. * The Language of Terrorism Since 9/11
  New Lines | 11 Sep 10:51 UTC
  While the world’s media have retreated from using the word "terrorism," governments have applied it ever more broadly, with vast implications for law and society. How does this simultaneous avoidance and expansion of the use of the word affect how we understand and address the phenomenon of violence committed in the name of an ideal? The ...
  FULL TEXT: The 20th century saw vast developments in the capacity of nations to kill, maim and control. But perhaps the single biggest shift in waging war was that from fighting on the ground to taking to the skies. Suddenly, weapons — whether ballistic or chemical — could come from above, tapping into ancient archetypes of fear. Violent death could come at any time, in any place, with minimal warning, fundamentally changing the theater of conflict. Anywhere could be — and was — targeted. Blitzes razed whole cities with no concern for who was there. Eventually, the technology brought nuclear weapons to the cities of Hiroshima and Nagasaki, which seemed to be the pinnacle of human destruction. But a hallmark of humans is to continually adapt, finding new ways to live — and to kill. Although the use of nuclear weapons has been constrained by human horror at the effects and by subsequent international treaties, there has been a proliferation of other threats from the skies. Drones also strike without warning, at a fraction of the financial cost of other airborne weapons; they also spy on everyday activity, inspiring paranoia and yet more fear. And, 25 years ago, passenger planes were used to inflict the largest one-day death toll inflicted by an enemy of the United States on American soil, with effects, both physical and psychological, that continue to this day. The 9/11 attacks soon came to symbolize terrorism, with images of planes penetrating the twin towers in downtown New York used to illustrate and convey the feeling of terror coming from the skies. But news organizations have moved away from using the word “terrorism” to describe the events of that day 25 years ago. From Reuters to the BBC, official guidelines state that best practice is to use descriptions of specific forms of violence (hijackings, bombings, knife attacks) rather than the label “terrorism,” arguing that the term itself is value-laden whereas the actions themselves are not. The ultimate aim of bearing witness through journalism, the guidelines explain, is best served by neutral language that does not include the judgment inherent in the word “terrorist.” Yet while the world’s media have retreated from the use of such language, governments have applied it ever more broadly, with vast implications for law and society. Terrorism legislation, especially that devised within the framework of the war on terror, uses definitions of terrorism vague enough to be expanded, seemingly indefinitely. From antif
  https://newlinesmag.com/essays/the-language-of-terrorism-since-9-11/

Experts believe AI companies’ focus is less on poaching from big tech firms and more on seeking individuals who have such experience. * Penchants of the polymaths
  Aeon | 11 Sep 10:00 UTC
  The fathers of Islamic science hold lessons for students: breadth over specialisation, and never let constraints get in the way - by Mariam Sabri Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: From Chronology of Ancient Nations ( c 1000 CE) by Abu Rayhan al-Biruni. Courtesy Edinburgh University , Scotland This semester, my students are learning about four polymaths who shaped Islamic science: al-Biruni, Alhazen, Avicenna and al-Khwarizmi. Their circumstances could hardly have been more different: one wrote his way out of scarcity; one was held under house arrest for a decade; one distilled an era’s medical knowledge into a text that ruled European universities for 500 years; and the last worked at the largest research institution of his age. But each shared the same underlying instinct: to move freely between mathematics, medicine, astronomy, philosophy and poetry, testing one discipline’s assumptions against another’s. My classroom is in Pakistan, where students arrive eager to talk about disruption and employability, but rarely about breadth. I want them to see, through these four lives, that this kind of expansiveness isn’t a luxury for stable times; it has produced some of the most enduring breakthroughs in the history of science. After 15 years working in liberal arts education, I believe a reintegration of disciplines in a Hellenistic style can lead the way. I’m not making a superficial case for ‘interdisciplinarity’. Instead, I’m arguing for something more specific: the recovery of the penchants of polymathy that once thrived in the Muslim world. Rather than fetishising disruptive innovation through compulsory courses on entrepreneurship and applied methods, we should offer an entirely different model of how to learn and what learning is for – especially since my students already enter university with a narrowly vocational mindset. The word polymathy itself captures the bricolage of many ( poly ) worlds of knowledge ( mathy ). Polymathy offers respite and reflection in a world fractured by war, climate catastrophe and injustice. It is sourced from indigenous idioms of the Muslim world rather than an imposition by the Global North. Today, polymathy brings different kinds of thinking to bear on political, economic and environmental problems. At a time when students are reading less of the classic canon and worrying more about employment opportunities, the expansiveness of polymathy can offer a meaningful path forward. These are the lessons of the great Islamic polymaths. T he life of our opening actor al-Biruni has much to teach students about the human dimensions of science.   FULL TEXT: In May 1992, I went on a real estate tour of suburban West Bank settlements. The tour was part of the government-sponsored “Operation Populate Judea and Samaria,” which referred to the biblical name of the territory known by most of the world as the West Bank. Most of the passengers on our bus were young couples trying to figure out how to afford a first home. In one settlement, they were offered 680-square-foot homes, with the legal right to expand their houses as their families grew, for a fraction of the price of a similar-sized city flat. An official told us, while we stood outside another settlement, that we could obtain a government mortgage for 95% of the house’s value, and that some of its value could be turned into a grant. In short, it was a giveaway. A crucial election was a month away. The ruling right-wing Likud Party, led by Yitzhak Shamir, feared that Yitzhak Rabin’s Labor Party would win, and would be willing to trade pieces of the occupied West Bank for peace with the Palestinians. Once Israeli citizens bought homes, though, it would be hard to keep them from moving in. Once they moved in, the political price of forcing them to leave would be too high to pay. “What if Rabin wins? What if they give it back?” a young man with a visibly pregnant wife asked our guide. “We don’t build here to give it back,” the guide answered. Rabin did win. He went on to sign the Oslo Accords, a supposedly interim agreement that turned over part of the West Bank to limited Palestinian rule — and which left all the settlements and much land between them under full Israeli control, their fate awaiting a final peace agreement that has yet to come. The “interim” arrangement has lasted ever since. Settlement building achieved its goal, which was to leave future Israeli governments fewer options for peacemaking. Today, Israel has a right-wing government more extreme and less inhibited than Shamir’s. Given current polling numbers, it has even more reason to be fearful about an election two months away. This fear is driving a frenzy of settlement activity in the West Bank. The aircraft that was about to land was an Italian government plane, organized by the country’s secret services. The man who stepped off the plane, smiling, was Gen. Osama Almasri Njeem. He had been arrested two days earlier by Italian authorities at a hotel in Turin, under a warrant issued by the International Criminal Court. The warrant charged him with the murder, torture, rape and enslavement of migrants detained at Mitiga Prison, located just a few hundred yards from the runway where he landed as a free man. How did a man who stood accused of such horrific crimes find himself freed by one of the states that helped establish the ICC? The Salafist-leaning group had been folded into the Government of National Unity (GNU) under Prime Minister Abdul Hamid Dbeibeh, and tasked with fighting organized crime and terrorism. In practice, the group largely controls Mitiga Airport and the adjacent prison, where thousands of detainees are held in brutal conditions, often without charges or a chance at legal proceedings. In reports to the United Nations Security Council, the Panel of Experts on Libya named Njeem among the commanders of an apparatus that had hijacked Tripoli’s justice system itself. None of this was unknown to Rome. Italy knew Njeem was on its soil and was aware of exactly who he was. The ICC had alerted Rome through diplomatic channels the day before his arrest. * Google Earth’s AI experiment lasted 24 hours. The damage to trust will linger
  Rest of World | 10 Sep 10:00 UTC
  A short-lived generative AI feature in Google Earth that allowed users to create fake satellite imagery shows the lasting effects of unvetted AI tools during a conflict.
  FULL TEXT: Since Israel and the U.S. launched strikes against Iran in February, plunging the Gulf region into conflict, the countries have also been waging an information war using tools powered by artificial intelligence. To separate fact from fiction, authorities, analysts, and journalists alike turned to commercial satellite imagery, including Google Earth, the closest thing to “ground truth,” with limited access to sites. Google Earth has long been a trusted infrastructure for the verification field, and is the platform on which open-source investigators are formally trained for conflict work in Syria, Yemen, and Sudan. Yet on July 30, Google launched a new feature that built image generation directly into Google Earth , the product that for two decades was synonymous with satellite image truth. New ways to fabricate launch overnight, a storm of criticism ensues, and guardrails arrive only after the harm.” It meant that users could zoom in to real coordinates, type a prompt, and get an AI-generated photorealistic scene blended into actual satellite and aerial imagery. Within hours, users had conjured a nuclear plant in Iran , a bomb crater beside a Gaza hospital , and floods lapping the U.S. Capitol. Faking satellite imagery is not new. Google’s own watermarks had exposed its Gemini AI model being used to fake satellite evidence, a recurring feature of Iran’s state-affiliated propaganda. But the Google Earth feature provided greater ease and accessibility inside the very tool that was known for trusted information. Within a day, following an outcry from investigative experts and the media, Google pulled the feature, saying it would return with “stronger guardrails.” That is our information environment in a nutshell: New ways to fabricate launch overnight, a storm of criticism ensues, and guardrails arrive only after the harm. Meanwhile, the U.S. government ordered satellite companies to restrict journalists’ access to imagery in the region, citing wartime security, narrowing the supply of real information even as the counterfeits spread. This was not the first generative AI product from a big tech company to be pulled or shelved recently. OpenAI shut down its Sora video app in March following sustained public demands for its withdrawal. But the recall came only months after a flood of abusive content of women and problematic recreations of historical figures. * Medieval mental health
  Aeon | 10 Sep 10:00 UTC
  Saffron and ostrich liver were prescribed but so were exercise, gardens, animals, socialising, and music - by Katherine Harvey Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: All images from the Codex Manesse (1300-40) and courtesy Heidelberg University Library Early one morning in the late 12th century, Bishop Hugh of Lincoln arrived in the Hertfordshire village of Cheshunt. There, he was immediately surrounded by a crowd of people, who begged him to help one of their neighbours. And so, dismounting from his horse, he went to the man’s house, where he was confronted by the disturbing sight of a possessed man ‘lying prostrate with his head tied to a post and each of his hands to great stakes fixed in the ground.’ Despite these restraints, the man constantly writhed around, rolling his eyes, gnashing his teeth, and sticking his tongue out. To Hugh, as to the local onlookers, the diagnosis was clear: this unfortunate sailor had been possessed by a demon and could be cured only by an exorcism. Once the necessary rituals were performed, he was ‘delivered from the demon [and] … after living devoutly for some years, finally made a good and peaceful end.’ For Hugh’s biographer, Adam of Eynsham, the incident was worth recounting because it demonstrated not only that the future saint had healing powers, but that he was a brave and compassionate man (in sharp contrast to the local bishop, who had ‘galloped away as if pursued by the Furies’, rather than face a possessed man). But, to us, the story – full of fear, cruelty and superstition – sums up both everything that was wrong with medieval attitudes to mental illness, and the apparent uselessness of medieval medicine in the face of serious illness. A nd yet, as is so often the case when studying the distant past, the reality was rather more complicated. Influenced by stories of demonic possession (and there are lots of them, especially in saints’ lives and miracle collections), I long assumed that our ancestors’ attitudes to, and experiences of, mental health were completely different to our own – and that they would have little sympathy with our fast-growing mental health crisis. But when I turned my attention away from religious texts, and began to research medieval ideas about healthy living, I soon came across medieval people who discussed mental health in ways that felt far more familiar to me. The similarities were particularly apparent in medical writings, which outline a sophisticated argument that mental wellness was at the heart of a healthy lifestyle.
